Sometimes I waste a good bit of time doing it when I should already be getting work done on the farm.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre you worried about anything else besides how your face and hands look?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 62 / miscellaneous_agricultural_worker / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. I’ve missed calls with bankers, and I’ve put off making decisions on the farm because I couldn’t get myself to feel like I looked acceptable enough to sit down with people. I keep thinking bankers or county people are noticing it, even though my wife tells me I just look like someone who’s worked outdoors a long time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face and hands would need to be perfect in order for you to feel comfortable around others?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say perfect, exactly. But I do feel like I’d have to look a lot better than I do to feel comfortable. I get stuck on how rough and worn my face and hands look—the cracks, the spots, the sagging—and I start thinking everybody, like bankers or county people, is staring at it. My wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, but it’s hard for me to believe that when I’m around people.\n\nDoctor: Is it the appearance of your face and hands in general that bothers you, or are you focused on specific flaws, like the cracks or spots?\n\nPatient: It’s both, I guess, but I do get fixed on the specific things—the cracks and spots on my hands, and the sagging or worn look in my face. It’s like once I notice them, I can’t get past it. I start thinking every banker or county person is seeing those exact things, even though my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if your face and hands looked better, people would treat you differently?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I do. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what brings me in is I can’t stop thinking about how rough and worn my face and hands look. The cracks, the spots, the sagging—when I’m around bankers or county office people, I feel like they’re staring right at it. My wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, but I can’t seem to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything repeatedly to try to deal with these concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I check myself a lot—more than I should. If I’m going to a loan meeting or setting up at a craft fair, I’ll look in the bathroom mirror, then the truck mirror, turning my hat one way and then another, trying to make my face look less… worn, I guess. And I catch myself looking at my hands next to the younger workers’ hands, comparing them. Sometimes I waste a good bit of time doing it when I should already be getting work done on the farm.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about anything else besides how your face and hands look?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’ve got normal worries—work, money, getting older—but the thing that gets stuck in my head is mostly my face and my hands. I’ve missed calls with bankers, and I’ve put off making decisions on the farm because I couldn’t get myself to feel like I looked acceptable enough to sit down with people. And I’ve stopped agreeing to have my picture taken for things that would help promote the retreat or craft sales. It’s embarrassing, but it does get in the way.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your face and hands that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly my face and hands. I get stuck on how rough and worn my face and hands look—the cracks, the spots, the sagging—and I start thinking everybody, like bankers or county people, is staring at it. My wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, but it’s hard for me to believe that when I’m around people.\n\nDoctor: Is it the appearance of your face and hands in general that bothers you, or are you focused on specific flaws, like the cracks or spots?\n\nPatient: It’s both, I guess, but I do get fixed on the specific things—the cracks and spots on my hands, and the sagging or worn look in my face. That’s what I can’t seem to get past—the cracks and spots on my hands, and my face looking saggy or worn out. I keep thinking bankers or county people are noticing it, even though my wife tells me I just look like someone who’s worked outdoors a long time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face and hands would need to be perfect in order for you to feel comfortable around others?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say perfect, exactly. But I do feel like I’d have to look a lot better than I do to feel comfortable. I get stuck on how rough and worn my face and hands look—the cracks, the spots, the sagging—and I start thinking everybody, like bankers or county people, is staring at it. My wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, but it’s hard for me to believe that when I’m around people.\n\nDoctor: Is it the appearance of your face and hands in general that bothers you, or are you focused on specific flaws, like the cracks or spots?\n\nPatient: It’s both, I guess, but I do get fixed on the specific things—the cracks and spots on my hands, and the sagging or worn look in my face. It’s like once I notice them, I can’t get past it. I start thinking every banker or county person is seeing those exact things, even though my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if your face and hands looked better, people would treat you differently?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I do. If my face and hands didn’t look so rough—if the cracks and spots weren’t there, and my face didn’t look so worn or saggy—I feel like people might not look at me the same way. I keep thinking bankers or county people are staring at those things, even though my wife says I just look like someone who’s worked outdoors.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are actually judging you because of how your face and hands look, or is it more that you're upset about the way they look to yourself?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, really. I’m upset by how they look myself, but when I’m around people I can’t get past the idea that they’re noticing it too. Like a banker or someone from the county is staring at the cracks and spots on my hands, or how worn my face looks. My wife tells me I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, but it doesn’t feel that way to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about the appearance of your face and hands if you were completely alone?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I would. Maybe it wouldn’t be as bad without bankers or county people around, but I’d still see it myself. I’d still be looking at my hands, the cracks and spots, and seeing how worn my face looks. I can’t really get past it, even when my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face and hands actually look as bad as you feel they do, or is it just how you feel about them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, part of me thinks they really do look that bad. My face looks worn and kind of saggy to me, and my hands look rough, with the cracks and spots. I know my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, but I can’t seem to see it that way. It feels worse than that to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I get stuck on how rough and worn my face and hands look—the cracks, the spots, the sagging—and I start thinking everybody, like bankers or county people, is staring at it. My wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, but it’s hard for me to believe that when I’m around people.\n\nDoctor: Is it the appearance of your face and hands in general that bothers you, or are you focused on specific flaws, like the cracks or spots?\n\nPatient: It’s both, I guess, but I do get fixed on the specific things—the cracks and spots on my hands, and the sagging or worn look in my face. It’s like once I notice them, I can’t get past it. I start thinking every banker or county person is seeing those exact things, even though my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if your face and hands looked better, people would treat you differently?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I do. If my face and hands didn’t look so rough—if the cracks and spots weren’t there, and my face didn’t look so worn or saggy—I feel like people might not look at me the same way. I keep thinking bankers or county people are staring at those things, even though my wife says I just look like someone who’s worked outdoors.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are actually judging you because of how your face and hands look, or is it more that you're upset about the way they look to yourself?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, really. I’m upset by how they look myself, but when I’m around people I can’t get past the idea that they’re noticing it too. Like a banker or someone from the county is staring at the cracks and spots on my hands, or how worn my face looks. My wife tells me I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, but it doesn’t feel that way to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about the appearance of your face and hands if you were completely alone?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I would. Maybe it wouldn’t be as bad without bankers or county people around, but I’d still see it myself. I’d still be looking at my hands, the cracks and spots, and seeing how worn my face looks. I can’t really get past it, even when my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face and hands actually look as bad as you feel they do, or is it just how you feel about them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, part of me thinks they really do look that bad. My face looks worn and kind of saggy to me, and my hands look rough, with the cracks and spots. I know my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, but I can’t seem to see it that way. It feels worse than that to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your appearance as frequently if you weren't going to be around other people?\n\nPatient: Probably not as much, no. It gets worse when I know I’m going to be around people, like before a loan meeting or if I’m setting up at a craft-fair table. I’ll check the bathroom mirror, then the truck mirror, turn my hat around a little, look at my hands again… sometimes I compare them to the younger workers’ hands. I end up wasting time I should be using on the farm. If I were staying by myself, I think I’d still look, but not over and over like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I think I’d still be anxious, yes. Maybe not as much if I truly knew nobody was judging me, but I can’t get past how rough and worn my face and hands look. [
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what brings me in is I can’t stop thinking about how rough and worn my face and hands look. The cracks, the spots, the sagging—when I’m around bankers or county office people, I feel like they’re staring right at it. My wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, but I can’t seem to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything repeatedly to try to deal with these concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I check myself a lot—more than I should. If I’m going to a loan meeting or setting up at a craft fair, I’ll look in the bathroom mirror, then the truck mirror, turning my hat one way and then another, trying to make my face look less… worn, I guess. And I catch myself looking at my hands next to the younger workers’ hands, comparing them. Sometimes I waste a good bit of time doing it when I should already be getting work done on the farm.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about anything else besides how your face and hands look?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’ve got normal worries—work, money, getting older—but the thing that gets stuck in my head is mostly my face and my hands. That’s what I keep coming back to.\n\nDoctor: Is this concern about your face and hands affecting your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. I’ve missed calls with bankers, and I’ve put off making decisions on the farm because I couldn’t get myself to feel like I looked acceptable enough to sit down with people. And I’ve stopped agreeing to have my picture taken for things that would help promote the retreat or craft sales. It’s embarrassing, but it does get in the way.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your face and hands that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly my face and hands. That’s what I can’t seem to get past—the cracks and spots on my hands, and my face looking saggy or worn out. I keep thinking bankers or county people are noticing it, even though my wife tells me I just look like someone who’s worked outdoors a long time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face and hands would need to be perfect in order for you to feel comfortable around others?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say perfect, exactly. But I do feel like I’d have to look a lot better than I do to feel comfortable. I get stuck on how rough and worn my face and hands look—the cracks, the spots, the sagging—and I start thinking everybody, like bankers or county people, is staring at it. My wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, but it’s hard for me to believe that when I’m around people.\n\nDoctor: Is it the appearance of your face and hands in general that bothers you, or are you focused on specific flaws, like the cracks or spots?\n\nPatient: It’s both, I guess, but I do get fixed on the specific things—the cracks and spots on my hands, and the sagging or worn look in my face. It’s like once I notice them, I can’t get past it. I start thinking every banker or county person is seeing those exact things, even though my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if your face and hands looked better, people would treat you differently?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I do. If my face and hands didn’t look so rough—if the cracks and spots weren’t there, and my face didn’t look so worn or saggy—I feel like people might not look at me the same way. I keep thinking bankers or county people are staring at those things, even though my wife says I just look like someone who’s worked outdoors.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are actually judging you because of how your face and hands look, or is it more that you're upset about the way they look to yourself?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, really. I’m upset by how they look myself, but when I’m around people I can’t get past the idea that they’re noticing it too. Like a banker or someone from the county is staring at the cracks and spots on my hands, or how worn my face looks. My wife tells me I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, but it doesn’t feel that way to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about the appearance of your face and hands if you were completely alone?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I would. Maybe it wouldn’t be as bad without bankers or county people around, but I’d still see it myself. I’d still be looking at my hands, the cracks and spots, and seeing how worn my face looks. I can’t really get past it, even when my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face and hands actually look as bad as you feel they do, or is it just how you feel about them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, part of me thinks they really do look that bad. My face looks worn and kind of saggy to me, and my hands look rough, with the cracks and spots. I know my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, but I can’t seem to see it that way. It feels worse than that to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your appearance as frequently if you weren't going to be around other people?\n\nPatient: Probably not as much, no. It gets worse when I know I’m going to be around people, like before a loan meeting or if I’m setting up at a craft-fair table. I’ll check the bathroom mirror, then the truck mirror, turn my hat around a little, look at my hands again… sometimes I compare them to the younger workers’ hands. I end up wasting time I should be using on the farm. If I were staying by myself, I think I’d still look, but not over and over like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel self-conscious about your face and hands if nobody else was around to see them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I would still feel self-conscious. It’s not only when someone else sees me. I get stuck on how rough my hands look, the cracks and spots, and how worn my face looks. Being around bankers or county people makes it worse because I feel like they’re noticing it, but even alone I still see it. My wife tells me I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, but I have a hard time believing that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious about your appearance if you were certain that others did not judge you based on how you look?\n\nPatient: I think I’d still be anxious, yes. Maybe not as much if I truly knew nobody was judging me, but I can’t get past how rough and worn my face and hands look. The cracks, the spots, the sagging… it just stands out to me. And when I’m around bankers or county people, it feels like they’re staring at it, even though my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what brings me in is I can’t stop thinking about how rough and worn my face and hands look. The cracks, the spots, the sagging—when I’m around bankers or county office people, I feel like they’re staring right at it. My wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, but I can’t seem to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything repeatedly to try to deal with these concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I check myself a lot—more than I should. If I’m going to a loan meeting or setting up at a craft fair, I’ll look in the bathroom mirror, then the truck mirror, turning my hat one way and then another, trying to make my face look less… worn, I guess. And I catch myself looking at my hands next to the younger workers’ hands, comparing them. Sometimes I waste a good bit of time doing it when I should already be getting work done on the farm.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about anything else besides how your face and hands look?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’ve got normal worries—work, money, getting older—but the thing that gets stuck in my head is mostly my face and my hands. That’s what I keep coming back to.\n\nDoctor: Is this concern about your face and hands affecting your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. I’ve missed calls with bankers, and I’ve put off making decisions on the farm because I couldn’t get myself to feel like I looked acceptable enough to sit down with people. And I’ve stopped agreeing to have my picture taken for things that would help promote the retreat or craft sales. It’s embarrassing, but it does get in the way.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your face and hands that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly my face and hands. That’s what I can’t seem to get past—the cracks and spots on my hands, and my face looking saggy or worn out. I keep thinking bankers or county people are noticing it, even though my wife tells me I just look like someone who’s worked outdoors a long time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face and hands would need to be perfect in order for you to feel comfortable around others?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say perfect, exactly. But I do feel like I’d have to look a lot better than I do to feel comfortable. I get stuck on how rough and worn my face and hands look—the cracks, the spots, the sagging—and I start thinking everybody, like bankers or county people, is staring at it. My wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, but it’s hard for me to believe that when I’m around people.\n\nDoctor: Is it the appearance of your face and hands in general that bothers you, or are you focused on specific flaws, like the cracks or spots?\n\nPatient: It’s both, I guess, but I do get fixed on the specific things—the cracks and spots on my hands, and the sagging or worn look in my face. It’s like once I notice them, I can’t get past it. I start thinking every banker or county person is seeing those exact things, even though my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if your face and hands looked better, people would treat you differently?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I do. If my face and hands didn’t look so rough—if the cracks and spots weren’t there, and my face didn’t look so worn or saggy—I feel like people might not look at me the same way. I keep thinking bankers or county people are staring at those things, even though my wife says I just look like someone who’s worked outdoors.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are actually judging you because of how your face and hands look, or is it more that you're upset about the way they look to yourself?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, really. I’m upset by how they look myself, but when I’m around people I can’t get past the idea that they’re noticing it too. Like a banker or someone from the county is staring at the cracks and spots on my hands, or how worn my face looks. My wife tells me I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, but it doesn’t feel that way to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about the appearance of your face and hands if you were completely alone?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I would. Maybe it wouldn’t be as bad without bankers or county people around, but I’d still see it myself. I’d still be looking at my hands, the cracks and spots, and seeing how worn my face looks. I can’t really get past it, even when my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face and hands actually look as bad as you feel they do, or is it just how you feel about them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, part of me thinks they really do look that bad. My face looks worn and kind of saggy to me, and my hands look rough, with the cracks and spots. I know my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, but I can’t seem to see it that way. It feels worse than that to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your appearance as frequently if you weren't going to be around other people?\n\nPatient: Probably not as much, no. It gets worse when I know I’m going to be around people, like before a loan meeting or if I’m setting up at a craft-fair table. I end up wasting time I should be using on the farm. If I were staying by myself, I think I’d still look, but not over and over like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel self-conscious about your face and hands if nobody else was around to see them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I would still feel self-conscious. Being around bankers or county people makes it worse because I feel like they’re noticing it, but even alone I still see it. Maybe not as much if I truly knew nobody was judging me, but I can’t get past how rough and worn my face and hands look. The cracks, the spots, the sagging… it just stands out to me. And when I’m around bankers or county people, it feels like they’re staring at it, even though my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to improve your appearance if it wouldn't change how others perceive you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I would. I guess… yes, I’d still want to improve it, even if those exact things couldn’t really be changed. It’s hard to explain, but I can’t get past how rough and worn my face and hands look. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what brings me in is I can’t stop thinking about how rough and worn my face and hands look. The cracks, the spots, the sagging—when I’m around bankers or county office people, I feel like they’re staring right at it. My wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, but I can’t seem to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything repeatedly to try to deal with these concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I check myself a lot—more than I should. If I’m going to a loan meeting or setting up at a craft fair, I’ll look in the bathroom mirror, then the truck mirror, turning my hat one way and then another, trying to make my face look less… worn, I guess. And I catch myself looking at my hands next to the younger workers’ hands, comparing them. Sometimes I waste a good bit of time doing it when I should already be getting work done on the farm.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about anything else besides how your face and hands look?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’ve got normal worries—work, money, getting older—but the thing that gets stuck in my head is mostly my face and my hands. That’s what I keep coming back to.\n\nDoctor: Is this concern about your face and hands affecting your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. I’ve missed calls with bankers, and I’ve put off making decisions on the farm because I couldn’t get myself to feel like I looked acceptable enough to sit down with people. And I’ve stopped agreeing to have my picture taken for things that would help promote the retreat or craft sales. It’s embarrassing, but it does get in the way.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your face and hands that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly my face and hands. That’s what I can’t seem to get past—the cracks and spots on my hands, and my face looking saggy or worn out. I keep thinking bankers or county people are noticing it, even though my wife tells me I just look like someone who’s worked outdoors a long time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face and hands would need to be perfect in order for you to feel comfortable around others?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say perfect, exactly. But I do feel like I’d have to look a lot better than I do to feel comfortable. I get stuck on how rough and worn my face and hands look—the cracks, the spots, the sagging—and I start thinking everybody, like bankers or county people, is staring at it. My wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, but it’s hard for me to believe that when I’m around people.\n\nDoctor: Is it the appearance of your face and hands in general that bothers you, or are you focused on specific flaws, like the cracks or spots?\n\nPatient: It’s both, I guess, but I do get fixed on the specific things—the cracks and spots on my hands, and the sagging or worn look in my face. It’s like once I notice them, I can’t get past it. I start thinking every banker or county person is seeing those exact things, even though my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if your face and hands looked better, people would treat you differently?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I do. If my face and hands didn’t look so rough—if the cracks and spots weren’t there, and my face didn’t look so worn or saggy—I feel like people might not look at me the same way. I keep thinking bankers or county people are staring at those things, even though my wife says I just look like someone who’s worked outdoors.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are actually judging you because of how your face and hands look, or is it more that you're upset about the way they look to yourself?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, really. I’m upset by how they look myself, but when I’m around people I can’t get past the idea that they’re noticing it too. Like a banker or someone from the county is staring at the cracks and spots on my hands, or how worn my face looks. My wife tells me I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, but it doesn’t feel that way to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about the appearance of your face and hands if you were completely alone?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I would. Maybe it wouldn’t be as bad without bankers or county people around, but I’d still see it myself. I’d still be looking at my hands, the cracks and spots, and seeing how worn my face looks. I can’t really get past it, even when my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face and hands actually look as bad as you feel they do, or is it just how you feel about them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, part of me thinks they really do look that bad. My face looks worn and kind of saggy to me, and my hands look rough, with the cracks and spots. I know my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, but I can’t seem to see it that way. It feels worse than that to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your appearance as frequently if you weren't going to be around other people?\n\nPatient: Probably not as much, no. It gets worse when I know I’m going to be around people, like before a loan meeting or if I’m setting up at a craft-fair table. I’ll check the bathroom mirror, then the truck mirror, turn my hat around a little, look at my hands again… sometimes I compare them to the younger workers’ hands. I end up wasting time I should be using on the farm. If I were staying by myself, I think I’d still look, but not over and over like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel self-conscious about your face and hands if nobody else was around to see them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I would still feel self-conscious. It’s not only when someone else sees me. I get stuck on how rough my hands look, the cracks and spots, and how worn my face looks. Being around bankers or county people makes it worse because I feel like they’re noticing it, but even alone I still see it. My wife tells me I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, but I have a hard time believing that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious about your appearance if you were certain that others did not judge you based on how you look?\n\nPatient: I think I’d still be anxious, yes. Maybe not as much if I truly knew nobody was judging me, but I can’t get past how rough and worn my face and hands look. The cracks, the spots, the sagging… it just stands out to me. And when I’m around bankers or county people, it feels like they’re staring at it, even though my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to improve your appearance if it wouldn't change how others perceive you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I would. Maybe it wouldn’t feel quite as urgent if I knew people weren’t judging me, but I still can’t get past how rough and worn my face and hands look. The cracks and spots and the sagging in my face… I keep feeling like I need to do something about it, even if my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to improve your appearance even if you couldn't change the specific things about your face and hands that bother you?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, I’d still want to improve it, even if those exact things couldn’t really be changed. It’s hard to explain, but I can’t get past how rough and worn my face and hands look. The cracks, the spots, the sagging—it’s what I see right away. And when I’m around bankers or county people, I feel like they’re looking right at it, even though my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.     "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Maybe some, yes. If I could really accept that this is just how I look after working outdoors all these years, I think it would take the edge off. But right now I can’t get past it. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what brings me in is I can’t stop thinking about how rough and worn my face and hands look. The cracks, the spots, the sagging—when I’m around bankers or county office people, I feel like they’re staring right at it. My wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, but I can’t seem to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything repeatedly to try to deal with these concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I check myself a lot—more than I should. If I’m going to a loan meeting or setting up at a craft fair, I’ll look in the bathroom mirror, then the truck mirror, turning my hat one way and then another, trying to make my face look less… worn, I guess. And I catch myself looking at my hands next to the younger workers’ hands, comparing them. Sometimes I waste a good bit of time doing it when I should already be getting work done on the farm.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about anything else besides how your face and hands look?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’ve got normal worries—work, money, getting older—but the thing that gets stuck in my head is mostly my face and my hands. That’s what I keep coming back to.\n\nDoctor: Is this concern about your face and hands affecting your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. I’ve missed calls with bankers, and I’ve put off making decisions on the farm because I couldn’t get myself to feel like I looked acceptable enough to sit down with people. And I’ve stopped agreeing to have my picture taken for things that would help promote the retreat or craft sales. It’s embarrassing, but it does get in the way.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your face and hands that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly my face and hands. That’s what I can’t seem to get past—the cracks and spots on my hands, and my face looking saggy or worn out. I keep thinking bankers or county people are noticing it, even though my wife tells me I just look like someone who’s worked outdoors a long time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face and hands would need to be perfect in order for you to feel comfortable around others?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say perfect, exactly. But I do feel like I’d have to look a lot better than I do to feel comfortable. I get stuck on how rough and worn my face and hands look—the cracks, the spots, the sagging—and I start thinking everybody, like bankers or county people, is staring at it. My wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, but it’s hard for me to believe that when I’m around people.\n\nDoctor: Is it the appearance of your face and hands in general that bothers you, or are you focused on specific flaws, like the cracks or spots?\n\nPatient: It’s both, I guess, but I do get fixed on the specific things—the cracks and spots on my hands, and the sagging or worn look in my face. It’s like once I notice them, I can’t get past it. I start thinking every banker or county person is seeing those exact things, even though my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if your face and hands looked better, people would treat you differently?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I do. If my face and hands didn’t look so rough—if the cracks and spots weren’t there, and my face didn’t look so worn or saggy—I feel like people might not look at me the same way. I keep thinking bankers or county people are staring at those things, even though my wife says I just look like someone who’s worked outdoors.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are actually judging you because of how your face and hands look, or is it more that you're upset about the way they look to yourself?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, really. I’m upset by how they look myself, but when I’m around people I can’t get past the idea that they’re noticing it too. Like a banker or someone from the county is staring at the cracks and spots on my hands, or how worn my face looks. My wife tells me I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, but it doesn’t feel that way to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about the appearance of your face and hands if you were completely alone?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I would. Maybe it wouldn’t be as bad without bankers or county people around, but I’d still see it myself. I’d still be looking at my hands, the cracks and spots, and seeing how worn my face looks. I can’t really get past it, even when my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face and hands actually look as bad as you feel they do, or is it just how you feel about them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, part of me thinks they really do look that bad. My face looks worn and kind of saggy to me, and my hands look rough, with the cracks and spots. I know my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, but I can’t seem to see it that way. It feels worse than that to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your appearance as frequently if you weren't going to be around other people?\n\nPatient: Probably not as much, no. It gets worse when I know I’m going to be around people, like before a loan meeting or if I’m setting up at a craft-fair table. I’ll check the bathroom mirror, then the truck mirror, turn my hat around a little, look at my hands again… sometimes I compare them to the younger workers’ hands. I end up wasting time I should be using on the farm. If I were staying by myself, I think I’d still look, but not over and over like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel self-conscious about your face and hands if nobody else was around to see them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I would still feel self-conscious. It’s not only when someone else sees me. I get stuck on how rough my hands look, the cracks and spots, and how worn my face looks. Being around bankers or county people makes it worse because I feel like they’re noticing it, but even alone I still see it. My wife tells me I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, but I have a hard time believing that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious about your appearance if you were certain that others did not judge you based on how you look?\n\nPatient: I think I’d still be anxious, yes. Maybe not as much if I truly knew nobody was judging me, but I can’t get past how rough and worn my face and hands look. The cracks, the spots, the sagging… it just stands out to me. And when I’m around bankers or county people, it feels like they’re staring at it, even though my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to improve your appearance if it wouldn't change how others perceive you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I would. Maybe it wouldn’t feel quite as urgent if I knew people weren’t judging me, but I still can’t get past how rough and worn my face and hands look. The cracks and spots and the sagging in my face… I keep feeling like I need to do something about it, even if my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to improve your appearance even if you couldn't change the specific things about your face and hands that bother you?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, I’d still want to improve it, even if those exact things couldn’t really be changed. It’s hard to explain, but I can’t get past how rough and worn my face and hands look. The cracks, the spots, the sagging—it’s what I see right away. And when I’m around bankers or county people, I feel like they’re looking right at it, even though my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your face and hands would lessen if you were able to accept them as they are?\n\nPatient: Maybe some, yes. If I could really accept that this is just how I look after working outdoors all these years, I think it would take the edge off. But right now I can’t get past it. My face and hands look so rough to me—the cracks, the spots, the sagging—and when I’m around bankers or county people, I feel like they’re staring at all of it. My wife tells me I just look like a man who’s worked outside, but it still bothers me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I think I probably would, yes. Even if you told me nobody else could see the cracks or spots, I still see them. That’s what I get stuck on—how rough and worn my face and hands look. I know my wife says I just look like someone who’s worked outdoors, but when I’m around bankers or county people, it still feels like that’s what they’re noticing.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what brings me in is I can’t stop thinking about how rough and worn my face and hands look. The cracks, the spots, the sagging—when I’m around bankers or county office people, I feel like they’re staring right at it. My wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, but I can’t seem to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything repeatedly to try to deal with these concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I check myself a lot—more than I should. If I’m going to a loan meeting or setting up at a craft fair, I’ll look in the bathroom mirror, then the truck mirror, turning my hat one way and then another, trying to make my face look less… worn, I guess. And I catch myself looking at my hands next to the younger workers’ hands, comparing them. Sometimes I waste a good bit of time doing it when I should already be getting work done on the farm.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about anything else besides how your face and hands look?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’ve got normal worries—work, money, getting older—but the thing that gets stuck in my head is mostly my face and my hands. That’s what I keep coming back to.\n\nDoctor: Is this concern about your face and hands affecting your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. I’ve missed calls with bankers, and I’ve put off making decisions on the farm because I couldn’t get myself to feel like I looked acceptable enough to sit down with people. And I’ve stopped agreeing to have my picture taken for things that would help promote the retreat or craft sales. It’s embarrassing, but it does get in the way.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your face and hands that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly my face and hands. That’s what I can’t seem to get past—the cracks and spots on my hands, and my face looking saggy or worn out. I keep thinking bankers or county people are noticing it, even though my wife tells me I just look like someone who’s worked outdoors a long time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face and hands would need to be perfect in order for you to feel comfortable around others?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say perfect, exactly. But I do feel like I’d have to look a lot better than I do to feel comfortable. I get stuck on how rough and worn my face and hands look—the cracks, the spots, the sagging—and I start thinking everybody, like bankers or county people, is staring at it. My wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, but it’s hard for me to believe that when I’m around people.\n\nDoctor: Is it the appearance of your face and hands in general that bothers you, or are you focused on specific flaws, like the cracks or spots?\n\nPatient: It’s both, I guess, but I do get fixed on the specific things—the cracks and spots on my hands, and the sagging or worn look in my face. It’s like once I notice them, I can’t get past it. I start thinking every banker or county person is seeing those exact things, even though my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if your face and hands looked better, people would treat you differently?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I do. If my face and hands didn’t look so rough—if the cracks and spots weren’t there, and my face didn’t look so worn or saggy—I feel like people might not look at me the same way. I keep thinking bankers or county people are staring at those things, even though my wife says I just look like someone who’s worked outdoors.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are actually judging you because of how your face and hands look, or is it more that you're upset about the way they look to yourself?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, really. I’m upset by how they look myself, but when I’m around people I can’t get past the idea that they’re noticing it too. Like a banker or someone from the county is staring at the cracks and spots on my hands, or how worn my face looks. My wife tells me I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, but it doesn’t feel that way to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about the appearance of your face and hands if you were completely alone?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I would. Maybe it wouldn’t be as bad without bankers or county people around, but I’d still see it myself. I’d still be looking at my hands, the cracks and spots, and seeing how worn my face looks. I can’t really get past it, even when my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face and hands actually look as bad as you feel they do, or is it just how you feel about them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, part of me thinks they really do look that bad. My face looks worn and kind of saggy to me, and my hands look rough, with the cracks and spots. I know my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, but I can’t seem to see it that way. It feels worse than that to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your appearance as frequently if you weren't going to be around other people?\n\nPatient: Probably not as much, no. It gets worse when I know I’m going to be around people, like before a loan meeting or if I’m setting up at a craft-fair table. I’ll check the bathroom mirror, then the truck mirror, turn my hat around a little, look at my hands again… sometimes I compare them to the younger workers’ hands. I end up wasting time I should be using on the farm. If I were staying by myself, I think I’d still look, but not over and over like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel self-conscious about your face and hands if nobody else was around to see them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I would still feel self-conscious. It’s not only when someone else sees me. I get stuck on how rough my hands look, the cracks and spots, and how worn my face looks. Being around bankers or county people makes it worse because I feel like they’re noticing it, but even alone I still see it. My wife tells me I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, but I have a hard time believing that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious about your appearance if you were certain that others did not judge you based on how you look?\n\nPatient: I think I’d still be anxious, yes.     "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think I would still feel anxious. Honestly, that’s kind of what worries me now — I feel like when I’m dealing with bankers or county people, they’re looking right at my face and hands, at the cracks and spots and the sagging. Even if those were the only things they noticed, that would still bother me a lot. My wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, but I can’t seem to get past how rough and worn I look.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what brings me in is I can’t stop thinking about how rough and worn my face and hands look. The cracks, the spots, the sagging—when I’m around bankers or county office people, I feel like they’re staring right at it. My wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, but I can’t seem to let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything repeatedly to try to deal with these concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I check myself a lot—more than I should. If I’m going to a loan meeting or setting up at a craft fair, I’ll look in the bathroom mirror, then the truck mirror, turning my hat one way and then another, trying to make my face look less… worn, I guess. And I catch myself looking at my hands next to the younger workers’ hands, comparing them. Sometimes I waste a good bit of time doing it when I should already be getting work done on the farm.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about anything else besides how your face and hands look?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’ve got normal worries—work, money, getting older—but the thing that gets stuck in my head is mostly my face and my hands. That’s what I keep coming back to.\n\nDoctor: Is this concern about your face and hands affecting your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. I’ve missed calls with bankers, and I’ve put off making decisions on the farm because I couldn’t get myself to feel like I looked acceptable enough to sit down with people. And I’ve stopped agreeing to have my picture taken for things that would help promote the retreat or craft sales. It’s embarrassing, but it does get in the way.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your face and hands that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about as well?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly my face and hands. That’s what I can’t seem to get past—the cracks and spots on my hands, and my face looking saggy or worn out. I keep thinking bankers or county people are noticing it, even though my wife tells me I just look like someone who’s worked outdoors a long time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face and hands would need to be perfect in order for you to feel comfortable around others?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say perfect, exactly. But I do feel like I’d have to look a lot better than I do to feel comfortable. I get stuck on how rough and worn my face and hands look—the cracks, the spots, the sagging—and I start thinking everybody, like bankers or county people, is staring at it. My wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, but it’s hard for me to believe that when I’m around people.\n\nDoctor: Is it the appearance of your face and hands in general that bothers you, or are you focused on specific flaws, like the cracks or spots?\n\nPatient: It’s both, I guess, but I do get fixed on the specific things—the cracks and spots on my hands, and the sagging or worn look in my face. It’s like once I notice them, I can’t get past it. I start thinking every banker or county person is seeing those exact things, even though my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if your face and hands looked better, people would treat you differently?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I do. If my face and hands didn’t look so rough—if the cracks and spots weren’t there, and my face didn’t look so worn or saggy—I feel like people might not look at me the same way. I keep thinking bankers or county people are staring at those things, even though my wife says I just look like someone who’s worked outdoors.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are actually judging you because of how your face and hands look, or is it more that you're upset about the way they look to yourself?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, really. I’m upset by how they look myself, but when I’m around people I can’t get past the idea that they’re noticing it too. Like a banker or someone from the county is staring at the cracks and spots on my hands, or how worn my face looks. My wife tells me I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, but it doesn’t feel that way to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about the appearance of your face and hands if you were completely alone?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I would. Maybe it wouldn’t be as bad without bankers or county people around, but I’d still see it myself. I’d still be looking at my hands, the cracks and spots, and seeing how worn my face looks. I can’t really get past it, even when my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face and hands actually look as bad as you feel they do, or is it just how you feel about them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, part of me thinks they really do look that bad. My face looks worn and kind of saggy to me, and my hands look rough, with the cracks and spots. I know my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, but I can’t seem to see it that way. It feels worse than that to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check your appearance as frequently if you weren't going to be around other people?\n\nPatient: Probably not as much, no. It gets worse when I know I’m going to be around people, like before a loan meeting or if I’m setting up at a craft-fair table. I’ll check the bathroom mirror, then the truck mirror, turn my hat around a little, look at my hands again… sometimes I compare them to the younger workers’ hands. I end up wasting time I should be using on the farm. If I were staying by myself, I think I’d still look, but not over and over like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel self-conscious about your face and hands if nobody else was around to see them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I would still feel self-conscious. It’s not only when someone else sees me. I get stuck on how rough my hands look, the cracks and spots, and how worn my face looks. Being around bankers or county people makes it worse because I feel like they’re noticing it, but even alone I still see it. My wife tells me I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, but I have a hard time believing that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious about your appearance if you were certain that others did not judge you based on how you look?\n\nPatient: I think I’d still be anxious, yes. Maybe not as much if I truly knew nobody was judging me, but I can’t get past how rough and worn my face and hands look. The cracks, the spots, the sagging… it just stands out to me. And when I’m around bankers or county people, it feels like they’re staring at it, even though my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to improve your appearance if it wouldn't change how others perceive you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I would. Maybe it wouldn’t feel quite as urgent if I knew people weren’t judging me, but I still can’t get past how rough and worn my face and hands look. The cracks and spots and the sagging in my face… I keep feeling like I need to do something about it, even if my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still want to improve your appearance even if you couldn't change the specific things about your face and hands that bother you?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, I’d still want to improve it, even if those exact things couldn’t really be changed. It’s hard to explain, but I can’t get past how rough and worn my face and hands look. The cracks, the spots, the sagging—it’s what I see right away. And when I’m around bankers or county people, I feel like they’re looking right at it, even though my wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your face and hands would lessen if you were able to accept them as they are?\n\nPatient: Maybe some, yes. If I could really accept that this is just how I look after working outdoors all these years, I think it would take the edge off. But right now I can’t get past it. My face and hands look so rough to me—the cracks, the spots, the sagging—and when I’m around bankers or county people, I feel like they’re staring at all of it. My wife tells me I just look like a man who’s worked outside, but it still bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel self-conscious about your appearance if you knew that the specific flaws you're concerned about, like the cracks and spots, were not noticeable to others?\n\nPatient: I think I probably would, yes. Even if you told me nobody else could see the cracks or spots, I still see them. That’s what I get stuck on—how rough and worn my face and hands look. I know my wife says I just look like someone who’s worked outdoors, but when I’m around bankers or county people, it still feels like that’s what they’re noticing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious about your appearance if you were certain that the cracks and spots on your hands and the sagging of your face were the only things that people noticed about you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I would still feel anxious. Honestly, that’s kind of what worries me now — I feel like when I’m dealing with bankers or county people, they’re looking right at my face and hands, at the cracks and spots and the sagging. Even if those were the only things they noticed, that would still bother me a lot. My wife says I just look like a man who’s worked outdoors, but I can’t seem to get past how rough and worn I look.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
